Put spending on an employee card, and both that individual and the primary cardholder earns status credit from the same spending. American lists this as valid through December 31, 2028 but that doesn’t mean it won’t be extended.

American’s business card earns redeemable miles that go into the company’s AAdvantage Business account, which are also transferable to individual AAdvantage accounts and you can choose where they go later.

I have the recently-refreshed Citi Executive card that now awards 10,000 additional Loyalty Points at each of four thresholds: 50,000, 90,000, 165,000 and 240,000 Loyalty Points in a qualifying year.

Those thresholds are triggered by Loyalty Points earned across your AAdvantage account, and doesn’t require spending a single dollar on the Executive card itself.

I’m a primary Citi Executive card holder. If I get one for my wife, then $105,000 on her business employee card would produce 105,000 Loyalty Points for each of us plus 20,000 in Executive card bonuses apiece. $105,000 spend becomes enough for two Platinum Pro and oneworld emerald statuses (without loyalty points from any other source, including Citi Globe’s ‘flight streak’ bonus).

And it offers a $100 inflight credit which covers food, beverages and Wi-Fi.

Spend $30,000 during your cardmembership year and renew, and you can earn a certificate for a companion’s eligible domestic Main Cabin roundtrip for $99 plus taxes and fees. The card also receives first checked bag free on eligible domestic itineraries and preferred boarding for the primary cardmember and up to four companions on the same reservation, 10% off eligible American Airlines Vacations packages, and no foreign transaction fees.

Wendell Berry on Where Our Loved Ones Gone Go

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

To love is to sign a contract with loss. To love anyway is both the great tragedy and the great triumph of life.

When two people have loved one another, each has become embedded in the marrow of the other’s being and both have had a profound experience that cannot be unhad even when the slender thread of grace between them is severed, by death or disenchantment. “No one you love is ever dead,” Hemingway wrote in his moving meditation on death and the meaning of life disguised as a letter of consolation to a bereaved friend.

Death, of course, is only one species of loss, one way our loved ones can leave us. We can also be left in life, left behind, left wondering where love goes when it goes. At its most discomposing, abandonment can feel like a living death.

All deep loss has one thing in common: It opens an absence so acutely felt it kidnaps us from presence, takes us away from our own lives and their insistent, inconvenient, sacred ongoingness. In memory or in fantasy, we end up refugees from reality, ceding the only thing that is ever ours for the keeping: the living moment. In all missing, we miss life.

That is what the achingly missed Wendell Berry (August 5, 1934–August 31, 2026) explores in this gasp of a poem, originally published in the November 17, 2025 issue of The New Yorker:

The loved ones we call the dead depart from us and for a while are absent. And then as if called back by our love, they come near us again. They enter our dreams. We feel they have been near us when we have not thought of them. They are simply here, simply waiting while we are distracted among our obligations. At last it comes to us: They live now in the permanent world. We are the absent ones.

Couple with physicist Richard Feynman’s extraordinary love letter to his wife written long after her untimely death, then revisit Wendell Berry on creativity and love and his timeless antidote to despair.

Something potentially significant could happen to Cardano (ADA) by January 1, 2027. Sam Altman’s ChatGPT AI predicts that  ADA may emerge as the standout token over the coming years, driven by a catalyst that could trigger a dramatic price increase.

This catalyst involves a coordinated decision by a consortium of emerging-market and African nations to adopt Cardano as the foundational blockchain for large-scale national digital identity systems, land registries, and public benefit distribution platforms.

Backed by significant international development funding and multi-year government contracts, this would position Cardano as critical public-sector infrastructure across multiple countries and drive sustained on-chain activity and demand for ADA.

The price continues to hold above key intermediate support levels while facing resistance around the $0.24–$0.26 zone. If digital identity and public services adoption accelerates across multiple nations, clearing key resistance could trigger powerful measured-move extensions and multi-cycle Fibonacci projections from the multi-year base.

Currently, momentum indicators are in neutral to oversold territory, suggesting there is considerable potential for a surge into the extreme overbought readings typical of parabolic advances. Key near-term support levels are around $0.21–$0.215 and $0.19–$0.20; maintaining these levels would keep the broader recovery structure intact.

Hope Is the Thing with Feathers, and with Fangs: The Alchemy of Unrequited Love and the Story Behind Emily Dickinson’s Most Famous Poem

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

This essay is adapted from the nineteenth chapter of my book Figuring.

In the first autumn of her thirties, Emily Dickinson wrote to her confidante and eventual editor Thomas Wentworth Higginson:

I had a terror — since September — I could tell to none, and so I sing, as the Boy does by the Burying Ground — because I am afraid.

Not a “fright,” not a “shock,” but a terror. What lay behind this enormity implied by a woman who measured her words so meticulously? Generations of biographers have filled pages with conjectures of varying persuasiveness — a death, some unrecorded heartbreak in her volcanic relationship with Susan, the first attack of epilepsy — but the most intriguing theory came nearly a century after the poet encrypted these words.

In 1951, after years of research and travel to various archives, the scholar Rebecca Patterson proposed a wholly novel candidate for the “terror” of 1861: Kate Scott Anthon — a newly widowed young woman Susan had befriended during their studies at the Utica Female Academy and then introduced to Emily, who fell into an intense romantic and possibly physical affair with the enticing newcomer before Kate severed the relationship without explanation, dealing a blow Emily would experience as deathly and furnishing the raw material for much of her mournful poetry.

Their story is a mosaic assembled from various surviving documents, as direct as Emily’s letters and as oblique as the marginalia in Kate’s favorite books.

In the late winter of 1859, Kate descended a sleigh in her fashionable black hat and widow’s veil in front of her former classmate’s home in Amherst. Almost immediately, Susan introduced her to the beloved auburn-haired friend who lived across the hedge in the brick house painted deep red and who had been hearing of her for nearly a decade. When Emily, wrapped in a merino shawl, met the tall, handsome woman with the penetrating dark eyes, musical voice, and lively passion for literature and astronomy, she was instantly entranced.

During the three weeks of Kate’s first stay in Amherst, the two women, both twenty-eight, became inseparable. They took long walks with Emily’s dog, Carlo, read Aurora Leigh aloud to each other, and spent evenings at the piano as Emily improvised — “weird and beautiful melodies, all from her own inspiration,” Kate would remember. As Emily played, Kate towered behind her — “Goliath,” the petite poet would call her.

When Kate left to go home, Emily beckoned her for another visit to Amherst:

I am pleasantly located in the deep sea, but love will row you out, if her hands are strong, and don’t wait till I land, for I’m going ashore on the other side.

Emily’s early letters to Kate pulsate with electricity. Writing weeks after they first met, she tries to disguise with playfulness the push-and-pull of irrepressible, frustrated longing in the code language of botany that was her first poetic tongue:

I never missed a Kate before. . . . Sweet at my door this March night another Candidate — Go Home! We don’t like Katies here! — Stay! My heart votes for you, and what am I indeed to dispute her ballot –? What are your qualifications? Dare you dwell in the East where we dwell? Are you afraid of the Sun? — When you hear the new violet sucking her way among the sods, shall you be resolute?… Will you still come?… Kate gathered in March! It is a small bouquet, dear — but what it lacks in size, it gains in fadelessness, — Many can boast a hollyhock, but few can bear a rose! … So I rise, wearing her — so I sleep, holding, — Sleep at last with her fast in my hand and wake bearing my flower. —

In the late winter of 1860, they spent a night together in Emily’s bedroom — unrecorded, inarticulable, except perhaps in verse:

Her sweet Weight on my Heart a Night Had scarcely deigned to lie — When, stirring, for Belief’s delight, My Bride had slipped away —

If ’twas a Dream — made solid — just The Heaven to confirm — Or if Myself were dreamed of Her — The power to presume —

Several weeks after that momentous night, Emily would channel this precious perishability in a letter to Kate:

Finding is slow, facilities for losing so frequent, in a world like this, I hold with extreme caution. A prudence so astute may seem unnecessary, but plenty moves those most, dear, who have been in want… Were you ever poor? I have been a Beggar.

Whatever took place between them, they never addressed it overtly — it is always impossible to articulate the possibility between two people, but especially in a time and place that confined the possible to such narrow parameters for permissible love. Feeling the impossibility of it all, Emily shuddered with anticipatory loss:

Kate, Distinctly sweet your face stands in its phantom niche — I touch your hand — my cheek your cheek — I stroke your vanished hair, Why did you enter, sister, since you must depart? Had not its heart been torn enough but you must send your shred?… There is a subject, dear, on which we never touch.

Little is known of Kate’s side of the experience. None of her letters to Emily survive. (The poet had instructed her sister that all letters be burned after her death — a request which Lavinia Dickinson promptly obliged before discovering the trove of poems that made her realize her sister’s correspondence might have immense literary value.) But Kate — who signed many of her surviving letters to other correspondents “Thomas” or “Tommy” — did have an unambiguous and lifelong proclivity for romantic attachment to women, culminating later in life with a longtime relationship with a young Englishwoman.

Perhaps at twenty-eight, she was simply not ready to so radically dismantle the superstructure of her life as she knew it. In April 1861, she severed the relationship with Emily. There is no record of what was said, but the devastation was complete and lifelong. Many years later, Emily would write to Higginson:

If ever you lost a friend… you remember you could not begin again because there was no world —

A breathless Death is not so cold as a Death that breathes.

In the immediacy of the loss, she interpolated between hope and despair, as we all do when discomposed by a sudden abandonment. A month after her “terror,” which might just be her painful acceptance that Kate was gone, her friend Samuel Bowles — whose newspaper had printed one of the only four poems published in her lifetime — came to Amherst. She refused to see him. Most of her letters from that period were burned, but Samuel was one of her most intimate friends — it is likely that she had confided in him the intensity of her heartbreak, if not its source. “We tell a Hurt to cool it,” she would write in a poem. Among his own letters is one from that summer to a recipient whose name has been scrubbed — an extraordinary letter of consolation to somebody anguishing with unrequited love, somebody who may well have been Emily:

… You must give if you expect to receive — give happiness, friendship, love, joy, and you will find them floating back to you. Sometimes you will give more than you receive. We all do that in some of our relations, but it is as true a pleasure often to give without return as life can afford us. We must not make bargains with the heart, as we would with the butcher for his meat. Our business is to give what we have to give — what we can get to give. The return we have nothing to do with… One will not give us what we give them — others will more than we can or do give them — and so the accounts will balance themselves. It is so with my loves and friendships — it is so with everybody’s.

Emily was not ready to let go of the love she had given, of the hope that it might one day be returned, though alchemised and transmuted into a different form. She wrote to Kate plaintively:

How many years, I wonder, will sow the moss upon them, before we bind again, a little altered, it may be, elder a little it will be, and yet the same, as suns which shine between our lives and loss, and violets.

That season, she composed her most famous poem — read here by twenty-first-century children who are yet to have their loves and losses, and animated by artist Olga Ptashnik:

“Hope” is the thing with feathers — That perches in the soul — And sings the tune without the words — And never stops — at all —

And sweetest — in the Gale — is heard — And sore must be the storm — That could abash the little Bird That kept so many warm —

I’ve heard it in the chillest land — And on the strangest Sea — Yet — never — in Extremity, It asked a crumb — of me.

“Life is long,” a poet friend said to me recently as I was reckoning with a similar rupture. But life was not long for Emily Dickinson, who died suddenly in her fifties, not a single grey on her auburn hair in the small white casket cradling her body and a posy of violets. Life is a feather borrowed from the swift wing of time. If she had lived longer, perhaps Kate would have returned to spend her remaining days with Emily and not with her English lover, or perhaps they would have met again in perfect disenchantment, in perfect friendship. “If” is the widest word of all, the immense alternate universe in which all of our possible lives live. Hope is what we call the bridge between this universe and that one.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. This is an embarrassment – Wouter Vrancken rages after Hearts humiliation

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Wouter Vrancken condemned a “ridiculous” first-half display from his sorry Hearts side as their 18-month unbeaten home record in the Scottish Premiership was ripped apart by ruthless Falkirk.

The beleaguered Jambos, who went into the game without 11 players through injury following a gruelling run of fixtures, endured a dismal afternoon as the Bairns raced into a 4-0 half-time lead on their way to a 5-2 victory at Tynecastle.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Finley Barbrook, Owen Eames, Liam Henderson and Jacob Murrell’s double had the visitors 5-0 up within 47 minutes before Hearts reduced the deficit through substitute Sabri Guendouz and a Claudio Braga penalty.

It was a first league defeat at Tynecastle since April 2025 for the Jambos, who have now won just two of their last 11 matches in all competitions.

“This is an embarrassment, the first half for sure,” said Vrancken. “Always doubting, always doing things half-half, in possession, out of possession, everything, and such easy goals again.

“When you give those easy goals, then you cannot win a game. It’s childish goals and cannot happen on this level. Those simple things, they are the basics actually in these situations.

“It’s crazy, crazy, and everybody – me, staff, players – have to take responsibility in those things. This is ridiculous.”

To exacerbate their grim predicament, the Jambos lost yet another senior player as defender Malachi Fagan-Walcott limped off in the second half.

“This also makes me a bit angry, because now we lose Mal again,” said Vrancken.

“Hopefully it’s not too bad, but still we had to replace him there, because he was one of the guys who were good in the game and in the last games actually he had a really consistent, good level.”

Hearts were leapfrogged into third place by Falkirk, who made it five top-flight wins in a row for the first time in 31 years.

“I’ve never been 5-0 up here before,” said Bairns manager John McGlynn. “First half we were outstanding. A great start to the game for us to get the goal so early.

“It was well documented about the Hearts injuries and, coming off the back of the game on Thursday night (in Thun), it was always going to be difficult for them.

“We had to smell blood and we had to go for it and that’s exactly what happened. The goals and our play were outstanding in the first half. We won every duel, we won everything all over the park.

“We got ourselves 4-0 up at half-time, which is dreamland. At 3pm, we never expected to be 4-0 up at half-time and certainly not 5-0 up a few minutes into the second half, which was another really good goal in transition.

“Hearts had a lot at the ball and dominated the second period, but we’re taking 5-2 every day of the week, make no mistake about it. It jumps us up into third place.

“Winning five on the trot has not been done since 1995, Jim Jefferies’ team, so I’m delighted for everyone at the football club that we’ve managed to equal that record.”

Spurs included one Ash Phillips clause that Middlesbrough won’t stop thinking about

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Middlesbrough are looking to end a 10-year exile from the Premier League at the end of this season, and are certainly well-placed to do so based on their early-campaign form.

Kim Hellberg was appointed at the Riverside Stadium 12 months ago, but has completely transformed the outlook of the North East side.

The Swede is now one of the most highly-rated head coaches outside the Premier League, whilst many of his squad on Teesside are in similar boats due to their current ability and exceptionally high potential.

Having fallen short in last season's automatic promotion race before losing May's play-off final to Hull City in crushing circumstances, Hellberg was significantly backed by Steve Gibson in the summer to make the desired changes in order to ensure such feats were a one-off.

As many as 12 new signings were recruited by Boro throughout the summer window, with depth and quality being added in equal measure right throughout their squad.

However, three of said dealings were particularly eye-catching in the form of Radek Vitek, Ash Phillips and Will Lankshear, with the latter duo both arriving in big-money moves from Tottenham Hotspur.

Spurs sold Will Lankshear to Boro in the summer, but, they have clauses written into the contract to protect their position in the future.

Phillips is no stranger to the Championship despite being just 21 years of age, having risen through the academy ranks at Blackburn Rovers before featuring 14 times for the Ewood Park outfit.

Spurs then activated a £2m release clause three summers ago, but the centre-back was swiftly loaned to Plymouth Argyle for the second period of 2023/24, with the Devon side narrowly avoiding relegation to League One.

Phillips then spent the past two seasons on loan at Stoke City, racking up 83 appearances in North Staffordshire, with his form and situation in North London subsequently attracting Middlesbrough's attention, whilst the Potters also sought a permanent move.

However, after a long-standing summer saga, during which talks were 'advanced' before hitting the buffers, the Salford native penned a five-year contract at the Riverside Stadium on August 15th.

Despite being so highly-rated, Phillips never made a senior appearance for the North London club, albeit the managerial shake-up which followed his arrival is likely to have contributed to such factors.

Roberto De Zerbi opted to cash in on the England youth international, who would otherwise be competing against the likes of Jan Paul van Hecke, Marcos Senesi and Micky van de Ven for a starting berth.

That said, Middlesbrough will certainly be wary of the fact Spurs still stand a chance of making their own significant funds if Phillips reaches his expected potential.

The aforementioned £7m initial deal is followed up by £13m in add-ons, meaning Spurs could make as much as £20m regardless, whilst also inserting a 20 percent sell-on clause within the agreement.

Spurs also have matching rights inserted into the deal, allowing them the potential to reunite with Phillips, irrespective of the fee another club puts on Middlesbrough's desk.

It remains to be seen what the future holds for Phillips, who has already become a mainstay in Hellberg's new-look defence as Middlesbrough set their sights on promotion.

The defender, no doubt, will eventually hope to make his mark in the Premier League too, and the Boro bosses' recent verdict only backs up why Spurs looked to protect themselves during negotiations.

"Ash has all the potential in the world to be as good a centre-back as there is," claimed Hellberg.

"The thing for him is, and that's why I think he's here, because the way we play challenges him to do the things he needs to do to become a Premier League centre-back.

"I don’t think he is at the moment, but I think in some aspects of the game, he's up with the top, top, top level," he added.

Things were going very well at the Stadio Luigi Ferraris against an in-form Genoa side, with Alphadjo Cisse and Christian Pulisic giving Milan a two-goal lead with less than 20 minutes on the clock. Both goals came from the high press, with Ruben Amorim’s side looking full of energy.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

However, as has been the theme this year, the Rossoneri were unable to turn it into a complete performance. The second-half drop-off saw Genoa build more and more pressure, with Ellertsson’s strike from range making it a nervy finish.

Milan held on to secure a win that gets them back into the top four for now, and the search for consistency can resume after the two-week break. Oli Fisher has five things were learned from the game at the Marassi.

We are running out of words to describe this debut season for Alphadjo Cisse. If it wasn’t certain from the sample size we had already, then the Bologna games and this one today were proof that he is a right-place, right-time player.

The young Italian was perfectly positioned for Rabiot’s pull-back in the opening exchanges, as he so often is. He ghosts into scoring areas and provides the killer touch, with the goal this afternoon being perhaps his most impressive so far: a first-time finish across his body right into the corner.

The cynical side of us wants to say that Goncalo Ramos could learn a thing or two about what spots to be in inside the box, though the defence of the Portuguese is that his movement often creates these pockets of space to attack.

Cisse is one of only three players born in 2006 or later to have scored 8+ goals across all competitions in Europe’s top five leagues. The others are Lamine Yamal (13) and Paris Brunner (8). He also matched Andriy Shevchenko’s eight goals in his first 13 games for the Diavolo.

Another remarkable stat is that the 20-year-old has scored his eight goals across all competitions from just 20 shots. Some might argue that kind of hit rate is unsustainable, but it shows no signs of stopping for now.

As mentioned in the introduction, the first-half performance from Amorim’s side was something that fans can really get behind. It was intense, aggressive and front-footed. The quality wasn’t always there to turn everything into danger, but the intent was there.

In fact, it was this tenacity that yielded the two goals. The first one came after Pulisic pressed and won back possession, allowing Rabiot to square for Cisse. Then, the press again forced an error from Genoa with Ostigard’s stray pass seized by Ramos, who squared for Pulisic to net.

It serves as categorical proof that something energy levels alone can be enough to get Milan into very good positions in games. Yet, we also saw the other side of things too: when the battery runs flat, the Rossoneri can lose control of games very quickly.

As a result of this, where more polished teams might see things out comfortably or even score more goals, Milan seem to sink into a hole of self-doubt, inviting pressure through a quite obvious inability to cycle the ball and keep opponents at bay.

Injuries are partly to blame for this, because not having versatile options like Fikayo Tomori, Samuel Chukwueze, Ruben Loftus-Cheek and Alexis Saelemaekers to bring on is hurting things. The bench on paper looked very weak, and those who did come in didn’t really help the chaos.

This is what Amorim refers to when he talks about the team not being ‘ready’. For various reasons – from the treatment room being full to the team not being fully cohesive – Milan’s level fluctuates wildly both within games and from match to match.

There was a report in La Gazzetta dello Sport this morning which highlighted a rather remarkable statistic. With Luka Modric, Mario Gila and Pulisic all not in the starting line-up, Milan are yet to pick up a single point.

This is arguably the crux of the rotation issue; the idea that managing the conditions of certain players is a necessity but upsetting the spine of the team is running a big risk. We’d like to add Rabiot and Diego Moreira into the mix too, as the five players Milan cannot do without.

The Spanish centre-back, the Croatian and French midfield duo, the Belgian wing-back and the American forward are simply too important. Their presence is vital not just because of their quality and propensity to provide decisive contributions, but because they are among the select group who can actually play Amorim’s way.

Each of them had a positive performance for various reasons. Gila’s fierce competitive nature facilitates the high line, Modric is the only one skilled in ball management, Rabiot is so wise when it comes to taking up the right positions, while Moreira and Pulisic are the technical sparks.

These are the players to build around from now until the end of season, while then waiting to see what the windows bring. When any combination of those five are playing, Milan’s limitations are often brutally exposed.

MilanNews described the performance from referee Davide Massa as a ‘disaster’, and we think that is being quite generous. The experienced official never once gave the impression of having control over a game where the pace and ferocity demanded it.

There is of course the one incident that everyone is talking about: the yellow card for Marcandalli after he brought Rabiot down. From our perspective, there are absolutely no doubts about it being a sending off.

The rules for DOGSO (Denial of a Goal Scoring Opportunity) take into account distance from goal, but the Frenchman and Cisse were about to have a two-on-zero attack with the entire half to run into. There is no way Ostigard was getting back to cover, and we question why VAR did not intervene.

To make matters worse, he then dished out a yellow card to Rabiot for rightfully protesting, and gave red cards to three of the Milan coaching staff too. In general Massa’s refereeing left a lot to be desired as there was no consistency in the fouls that he was whistling, and the five yellow cards the Rossoneri got felt disproportionate to Genoa’s two.

Thankfully the Diavolo were able to get over the line and bank the win, but if things had taken a more negative turn in the second half, we would be talking about that Marcandalli incident with a lot more venom.

Koni De Winter showed up in a big way against his old club, putting in another top performance in a week where he has been linked with a move to three big Premier League clubs.

Yet, we have to question the highs and lows. To best illustrate this, here are our ratings for the Belgian from each of the last four games.

vs. Bologna: Where to begin? I think he could have done better positioning-wise on Bologna’s first, with no one else to mark in the box, and then he completely gifted the free-kick that made it 2-1 for the guests. Some good moments too, but too many mistakes tonight. Maybe a 4.5?

vs. Inter (8): He absolutely won the battle against Thuram this game and it’s not like it was an easy one either. The two had several 50-50 duels and he always came out on top. Aside from his Thuram battle, he was very good overall and deserves a high rating here.

vs. Ferencvaros (5.5): That backpass for Torriani should never be played in that way, even if Maignan is between the sticks. He admittedly got a bit unlucky with his clearance in the second half, but even then perhaps he could have done better. Anyway, for me it’s the pass which brings his rating down.

vs. Genoa (7.5): The only poor moment came inside the first 60 seconds, when he was caught out a bit by a ball over the top, but he managed to track back and at least narrow the angle. After that, he was brilliant in his positioning, showing off with some great tackles and interceptions too. Really good.

Being the central centre-back is a position that carries a lot of weight and responsibility in Amorim’s system, and what is clear is that one game De Winter can look like the perfect man for the job, and in others he looks simply unplayable (and not in the good way).

One of the reasons for these wild fluctuations could also be because the former Juventus man simply hasn’t had a break. So, it is easy to see why the management are targeting a central defender ahead of January, someone who can give everyone a breather and help keep the levels high.

Elon Musk’s Grok AI Predicts an Unbelievable BTC Price

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Elon Musk’s Grok AI predicts an astonishing Bitcoin price by January 1, 2027. It forecasts a price range so extreme it would redefine Bitcoin’s role in the global financial system if a full-blown cryptocurrency bull market emerges alongside a major catalyst.

As of November 8, 2026, Bitcoin is trading between $75,800 and $76,000, according to recent CoinGecko data. This optimistic outlook is based on a strong bull market in late 2026, alongside a transformative catalyst.

In this scenario proposed by Grok AI, sovereign nations and institutions could coordinate a wave of adoption. Multiple countries and major central banks might begin treating Bitcoin as a strategic reserve asset. Additionally, new capital and reserve regulations could incentivize or require large-scale Bitcoin holdings as a non-sovereign store of value and collateral.

Under this speculative scenario, Bitcoin could surpass its previous all-time high of nearly $126,000 and enter a genuine price-discovery phase, driven by structural demand that goes far beyond typical flows from ETFs or corporate treasuries.

Bitcoin has been consolidating in a broad range after retreating from earlier attempts to sustain levels above $85,000–$90,000. The price continues to defend key support zones after recovering from lower lows near the mid-$70,000s.

In a standard bull market, a sustained breakout above the $85,000–$90,000 range would pave the way toward a retest of the previous all-time high near $126,000.

In an extreme scenario involving widespread sovereign reserve adoption and regulatory incentives for large-scale BTC holdings, surpassing that previous high could trigger significant measured-move extensions and multi-cycle Fibonacci projections from the multi-year base.

This could theoretically support a move into the hundreds of thousands of dollars, provided volume and institutional (and sovereign) investment surge.

The Relative Strength Index (RSI) currently sits in neutral-to-weak territory on shorter timeframes, with significant room to move into the extreme overbought levels typically seen in parabolic advances.

Key near-term support levels are found in the $74,000–$75,500 and $72,000–$73,000 zones. Holding these levels would help maintain the broader recovery structure while the market adjusts to any major narrative changes.

Overall, while the current chart suggests meaningful upside in a typical bull market, only an extraordinary surge in structural demand from sovereign and institutional reserve allocation could justify the kind of multi-fold extension implied by the most aggressive targets.

Michael Saylor has publicly declared that Bitcoin’s next bull market has already begun, citing the reclaim of key long-term moving averages and expanding digital credit and treasury demand.

Analyst consensus has also shifted sharply higher, with a large majority of surveyed analysts turning bullish, levels of optimism not seen since mid-2025.

On-chain and market-structure metrics from firms such as CryptoQuant have confirmed a transition into early-bull conditions after Bitcoin reclaimed its 365-day moving average.

While Bitcoin (BTC) bulls expect a parabolic price movement driven by institutional adoption, Bitcoin Hyper (HYPER) is focusing on another significant opportunity: bringing high-speed financial applications to the Bitcoin ecosystem.

As the largest cryptocurrency by market capitalization, Bitcoin attracts a vast audience of holders and investors. Bitcoin Hyper aims to leverage this liquidity through a Layer 2 network designed to enable faster transactions and decentralized finance (DeFi) functionalities.

Its architecture utilizes the Solana Virtual Machine (SVM), incorporating Solana-style execution capabilities into the Bitcoin-focused ecosystem.

Bitcoin Hyper’s goal is clear: to merge Bitcoin’s substantial liquidity and established investor base with transaction speeds tailored for modern decentralized applications. Key features include near-instant transaction finality and low costs, which are central to its positioning.

HYPER is attracting attention through its presale, which has reportedly raised over $33M, demonstrating strong early fundraising momentum.

It offers a 35% annual percentage yield (APY) for staking rewards, providing another incentive for early participants. Token holders can stake their HYPER tokens to accumulate additional rewards instead of leaving their allocation idle.

With the planned launch date of November 26 approaching, the presale is entering a crucial phase. Those interested in Bitcoin’s expanding Layer 2 ecosystem are looking forward to Hyper’s market debut before it lists on centralized exchanges (CEXs).

Democrats amp up Trump investigation threats, Blanche counters he may probe lawmakers

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Top Democrats are ramping up oversight of the Trump administration and businesses it has partnered with, while Attorney General Todd Blanche warned he could counterinvestigate lawmakers conducting probes.

Democrats romped to majorities in both the House and the Senate during Tuesday's midterm elections, putting them in position to seize control of committee gavels and subpoena power in January. The electoral rout leaves Democrats with at least 54 Senate seats and 254 seats in the House of Representatives as votes continue to be counted in several states.

Democrats vowed to investigate both President Donald Trump's White House and businesses that have worked with the administration. CNBC reported on Thursday that Democrats are expected to take an aggressive approach to probing businesses, especially those companies with ties to the Trump family. The technology, cryptocurrency and prediction market industries, among others, could immediately be in the crosshairs.

"If you've been making your profits by cheating American families and scamming your investors, you better get ready for some investigations — serious ones," said Sen. Elizabeth Warren, the incoming chair of the Senate Banking Committee, on CBS' "Face the Nation" on Sunday. "The American people are just damn sick of this."

Blanche, Trump's former personal attorney, whom the Senate narrowly confirmed as the nation's top cop earlier this year, took umbrage at the Democrats' plans for investigations and said he may launch his own investigations against members of Congress.

"There could be," Blanche said Sunday when pressed on CNN's "State of the Union" about whether the administration will counterinvestigate Democrats.

"When the threats become so vile and so dangerous that you are telling people in college that they better be careful if they want to work in the Trump administration because somebody will come after them, at some point you have to draw the line."

Blanche was referring to comments made by Sen. Elissa Slotkin, D-Mich., who said during a University of Chicago event that she wants "young people coming out of school to say, 'Should I go work for the Trump administration?'...I want them to be like, 'Lord, that might be buying myself a ton of problems that I don't want. And so let me avoid participating in that.' I want that deterrence message sent."

Trump has ordered the Justice Department to investigate Democrats over the comments.

Blanche also took aim at Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the likely incoming chair of the House Oversight Committee — appearing to caution lawmakers who sit on the panel. Garcia warned the administration and business leaders to "lawyer up" after the election.

"I would be very worried if I was a member on that committee," Blanche said.

Garcia shot back, saying on CNN: "What Todd Blanche is doing right now is a threat."

"He's trying to stop us from doing real oversight," Garcia said. "He is the person that should be reading the Constitution, because the Constitution makes it very clear that it's our job in Congress to conduct oversight of the executive and the administration."

Crystal Palace: Six managers who’d keep Eagles up as Sage sack decision beckons

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Pierre Sage’s time as Crystal Palace manager could be up after a 3-1 loss to Liverpool sank them to the bottom of the Premier League table.

Sunderland beating Chelsea means Palace have been overtaken in the relegation zone and now sit in last spot. Sage’s side have only won one of their last eight games in all competitions and it’s now four losses on the bounce in the league.

The international break is looming and it could be the perfect time for Palace to make a change in the dugout. But who could they attract?

Here are some reasonably realistic options that could get Palace out of their rut and potentially keep them up.

One of the most appealing names out there in terms of available managers with a respectable body of work in the Premier League.

The last time Howe was an available manager, after leaving Bournemouth in 2020, he took his time to get the right next job. But when Newcastle came calling in 2021, it turned out to be a match made in heaven.

There were highs and lows in Howe’s time at St. James’ Park as he became the face of a new era under new owners. But he was like a breath of fresh air for the Newcastle faithful who’d grown weary of what was being served up by Steve Bruce.

Howe eventually led Newcastle to their first trophy in decades by winning the EFL Cup in 2025. His reign also included a pair of Champions League qualifications.

His time with Newcastle proved he knows how to take a club struggling at the wrong end of the table and lead them to new heights.

Sure, he wouldn’t have the same kind of financial backing at Palace as he did at Newcastle, but Howe didn’t need it at Bournemouth and worked wonders there.

The only question is whether he’d see Palace as an opportunity or a step below him – especially if there’s a vacancy at Manchester United…

Again, this might be an ambitious one, but there’s not too much by the way of fresh opportunities for Slot right now after his sacking by Liverpool earlier this year.

It doesn’t seem to be bothering him too much. He recently went on a trip to South America to take in some games at a handful of iconic venues. He’s also believed to have said no to Fulham, AC Milan, Al-Ahli and the Netherlands since leaving Liverpool.

But his reason for rejecting his national team’s job was because of an itch to be coaching day in, day out. Now he’s had a few months out of the game, could he be more tempted now if a club like Palace gave him that chance again?

Palace actually had a look at him in 2023, reportedly making contact with the then-Feyenoord boss when they were looking for a long-term replacement for Patrick Vieira.

Slot, already an Eredivisie winner with Feyenoord by that point, boosted his reputation by winning the Premier League with Liverpool at the first time of asking. His stock dropped with a feeble title defence, but Palace are the kind of club he could prove himself with again.

If dreaming of Howe or Slot might be a bit above Palace’s station right now, then it might be time to press the Dyche button.

You know what you’re getting with Dyche and his tactics. Organisation. Discipline. Back to the basics.

Palace have tried to go for more adventurous styles in the past but have sometimes found it better when they’ve gone down the kind of route that Dyche would symbolise.

It may not have worked out for him at Nottingham Forest last season, but the situation there was a mess. It wasn’t all his fault.

At 55, Dyche still has something to give in management if he fancies it. It might not be the most attractive option, but of the realistic ones, it could be one of the most sensible.

If Howe or Slot would be too ambitious and Dyche wouldn’t be ambitious enough, Frank might be the happy medium.

His work with Brentford was excellent, helping to establish them as the stable Premier League club they are today. There were times during his reign when he’ll have had to look over his shoulders – and he steered the ship in the right direction.

Tottenham fans will have a different view of Frank’s ability, but if he wants to prove that was just a blip, Palace could be the kind of club to help him get back on track.

Give him time and Frank should be able to deliver some better results, all while building a stable project.

Pereira’s success at keeping Forest up last season should not be overlooked. He was an important factor in them ending the season strongly enough to avoid the danger of the drop.

There’d be a degree of irony if he was to take the Palace job after being replaced by Oliver Glasner, who of course was Sage’s direct predecessor at Selhurst Park.

Ask Palace fans and they’d surely rather have Glasner back after the cup-winning success he enjoyed with them, but that doesn’t look possible right now.

Pereira isn’t a manager to be dismissed from a shortlist so easily. Is he capable of building a long-term project? Maybe not. But he gave Forest a spark when they needed it last season and that’s what Palace could do with now.

Look, it’s (probably) not going to happen. The man’s retired. Numerous times.

But when Palace have been in bother before, Hodgson has consistently come to their rescue.

He hasn’t managed since his encore of a spell with Bristol City earlier this year and is unlikely to do so again.

For that and various other reasons, it’d be seen as a step backwards to revert to him in a caretaker role again, but if you’re wanting a manager who can keep Palace up, he’s been there and done that.

Manchester United fans made their feelings unmistakably clear at Old Trafford on Sunday, erupting into a loud cheer when full-back Diogo Dalot was substituted off during a chaotic afternoon against Aston Villa.

The reaction from the Old Trafford crowd was as brutal. When the board went up in the 66th minute indicating Dalot’s number was being replaced by Patrick Dorgu, a noticeable wave of cheers echoed through the stands.

The loud ovation was not one of appreciation, but rather sarcasm filled frustration after another poor defensive display.

Dalot’s tough afternoon began early as United found themselves trailing 2-0 within four minutes following goals from Alysson and Nicolas Jackson.

After United fought back to tie the game at 2-2 through Bruno Fernandes and Youri Tielemans before the break, Dalot’s second half dissolved into disaster.

In the 47th minute, the Portuguese defender committed a clumsy foul on Alysson inside the penalty area, allowing John McGinn to convert from the spot.

Six minutes later, Leon Goretzka bundled home a fourth for Villa, leaving Dalot completely exposed and compelling Michael Carrick to make the change.

The substitution proved to be game changer. Trailing 4-2, United mounted a heroic late rally, coming back from a two-goal deficit for the second time in the match.

Dalot’s replacement Dorgu was at the heart of the comeback, with a goal and assist.

Here are the best electric bikes you can buy at every price level in November 2026

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

I’ve spent countless hours here at Electrek doing detailed hands-on testing of hundreds of electric bikes. Through thousands of miles of riding, I’ve been fortunate to learn these e-bikes inside and out, top to bottom and front to back. That long-term experience with real-world e-bike testing has helped me find the best electric bicycles on the market for just about any budget.

Below are some of the top e-bikes I’ve hand-tested for every price range, current as of November 2026. These are probably the best deals you’ll find all year on electric bikes, meaning this is the best time ever to get a new model! Check out the awesome e-bikes below, any one of which could become your next electric bike.

This list is divided into several price ranges. We’ll start with the best e-bikes under $1,000, then move on to the under-$2,000 category, under-$3,000 category, etc. The goal is to highlight some of my favorite models in various budget ranges to help you make the right decision based on your needs and budget.

The sub-$1,000 electric bike category is critical for a few reasons.

First, it’s pretty hard to produce a quality e-bike and sell it for under $1k. Most of the options in this price category make too many sacrifices in quality or longevity to earn a spot on this list as one of “the best.”

Second, many newly curious e-bike buyers just aren’t prepared to drop several thousand dollars on a brand-new concept, making this price range critical for many new e-bike shoppers.

Fortunately, we’ve found some excellent options for cheap electric bikes that can still save you some serious dough.

The Lectric XP LITE 3.0 was recently updated and is now quite likely the king of bang-for-your-buck electric bikes.

For a while now, the $999 Lectric XP 4 has reigned supreme in the budget electric bike category. That’s still a great bike, and we’ll discuss it next, but the more affordable $799 Lectric XP LITE3 is an even more wallet-friendly electric bike.

That new e-bike offers some great riding at under 800 bucks.

It’s a throttle-enabled Class 2 folding electric bike with 20″ wheels, a max speed of 20 mph (32 km/h), and a motor rated for 300W continuous and 819W peak. It’s not going to be the strongest hill climber in this list, but it does get going quickly on flat and modestly hilly terrain.

And the biggest features include quality hydraulic disc brakes, color LCD screen, turn signals, and even an optional Gates carbon belt drive system for an extra $100. Riders can also opt for a nearly double-sized battery for an extra $200.

This is the only e-bike in this price range that I know of with a 48V electrical system. All the other budget e-bikes under $800 use a 36V system and thus suffer from weaker power.

There are some things that you give up with this model, such as that the fenders aren’t included free. But for just $799, you’re still getting built-in lights, a hidden battery, large color LCD display and a lightweight folder. Plus it comes in lots of fun color options.

The Lectric XP4 is the undisputed best-selling electric bike in the US. The bike has racked up massive sales figures as riders flock to its extreme bang-for-your-buck. Simply put, it’s hard to find any other e-bike that offers this level of value!

The XP4 can get riders up to 28 mph (45 km/h) and now comes in either a 500W or 750W version, with respective battery sizes of 48V 10.4Ah and 48V 17.5Ah. The long-range version’s premium price tag ($1,299 vs the standard range model at $999) is perhaps even higher value than the already impressive base model.

Other upgrades include a new stronger rack design, torque sensor for smoother pedal assist, and improved hardware like the 8-speed transmission, hydraulic disc brakes, updated urban/trail hybrid-style tire tread, and a beautiful new TFT color screen.

The XP4 is hands-down the highest value e-bike I’ve ever tested and it’s quite obvious just how much time and effort went into building such a potent e-bike designed to fit so many different riders and tasks.

It’s hard to find a better combination of low cost and high performance anywhere in the e-bike market right now.

When Ride1Up launched the original Portola, it was pretty obvious what the game plan was: fire a shot across Lectric Ebike’s bow with a direct competitor to the Lectric XP line. By all accounts, Ride1Up nailed it with an impressive competitor. Now the company has come back with the next generation of the bike, this time attempting to take on both the XP line and XP Lite from Lectric. That’s thanks to two models, the Portola 02, and the stripped-down Portola 02 Lite.

The Ride1Up Portola 02 has a lot more going for it than just the attractive starting price of $895 for the Lite or $995 for the full-size bike with frame-integrated rack. The models offer great performance with a maximum speed of 28 mph (45 km/h) and good quality components including hydraulic disc brakes, torque sensor, front suspension, a built-in rear rack (except for the Lite), and a 750W motor.

There are two battery options available, either a 48V 10Ah or a 48V 15Ah, and the larger battery only costs an extra $100, so it’s quite worth the extra 50% range.

I love the sleek-looking frame and apparently, I’m not alone. Those who are on the hunt for a good-looking folding fat tire e-bike that doesn’t break the bank will likely find this to be one of the more fashionable options.

Once you bump up above the $1,000 limit, you start to see many more great options for e-bikes in a wide range of categories.

From electric cargo bikes to cruisers and even electric mopeds, there’s something for everyone in this price range.

The $1,249 JackRabbit OG2 is in one of these fuzzy areas between e-bikes and other e-rideables. In this case, it straddles the line between an electric bike and an electric scooter.

It more or less feels like an electric bike based on its ride geometry. However, the lack of pedals means that it could also be classified as an electric scooter, albeit a large-wheeled e-scooter.

But with those 20-inch wheels and bicycle seat, the JackRabbit OG2 rides much more like a small folding e-bike than an e-scooter. And its electronics match that of a light e-bike, too.

The 300W motor gets the little runabout up to 20 mph (32 km/h). The small 158 Wh battery only offers around 10 miles (approximately 16 km) of range, but is also conveniently small enough to easily keep a spare battery in a cargo pocket or even a small purse.

The biggest benefit of the JackRabbit is that it is so simple that the lack of frills keeps the weight down to just 25 lb (11.3 kg)! For an e-bike, that’s almost so light that it floats.

The company also released a higher power and larger battery capacity version too, which might be good for riders who want to push harder and go farther. For those that want to see a few more upgrades on this minimalist e-bike, the JackRabbit OG2 and XG might be more your size.

See my full review of the JackRabbit OG2 here.

We called the Lectric XPedition e-cargo bike a runaway success right from the start, and that’s exactly what it turned into. Now with the new Lectric XPedition 2.0 on the road, it’s better than ever.

With heavy-hauling weight capacity, the option for one or two batteries for extra long range, hydraulic disc brakes and torque sensor-based intuitive feeling pedal assist, this e-bike is dragging electric cargo bikes into the future.

The $1,399 Lectric XPedition 2.0 has turned the electric cargo bike market upside down, bringing a heavy-hitting cargo e-bike to market for an ultra-affordable price.

While it’s not the fanciest cargo e-bike on the market (you’ll find those in the $4,000-$6,000 price range further down this article), it’s definitely the best bang for your buck thanks to a combination of high value parts and reasonable pricing.

The Heybike Ranger 3 Pro is probably one of the best deals in full-suspension folding e-bikes right now. With a combination of a powerful motor, comfortable geometry, front & rear suspension, and a high-tech interface that includes NFC card unlocking, the Heybike Ranger 3 Pro is punching above its weight class.

In fact, that’s its main drawback, its weight. It’s a heavy bike, but the speed and power mean heavier motors and batteries, so you know you’re going to be carrying a more robust powertrain around with you.

But between the performance and the looks, this is one awesome comfortable ride that just doesn’t break the bank! I had a blast reviewing it, and I’ll bet you’ll have a blast riding it!

The $1,399 Aventon Soltera.2 is the latest version of Aventon’s beautiful city bike that offers some of the best quality fabrication that I’ve seen in this price range – or even in higher price ranges. It is offered in either a single-speed or seven-speed format.

The bike rolls on narrow tires for that efficient city ride, while still packing in a 500W motor.

The battery is a bit smaller at 36V and 10Ah, but the bike makes up for a smaller battery by being able to integrate it into the frame in a sleek and attractive package.

The beautiful color display also adds to the bike’s charm, as do the powerful headlight and frame-integrated tail light. There’s even an awesome companion app that lets your phone track your bike’s important stats. Basically, you’re getting a lot for $1,199 here!

Check out my in-depth Aventon Soltera.2 review here.

Ride1Up has proven itself as a high-value electric bike brand, focusing largely on efficient and speedy commuter e-bikes. While the company has many models in the $1,000 to $2,000 range, the $1,295 Ride1Up Roadster V3 is one of the best bang-for-your-buck options in the lightweight category.

This 500W-rated e-bike offers Class 3 performance, meaning you can hit speeds of up to 28 mph (45 km/h), or a still impressive 20 mph (32 km/h) if using the throttle. But sticking to pedal-assist means you get to enjoy that quality torque sensor for smoother pedaling. The bike even comes with an option for either a belt drive or a 10-speed Shimano transmission, and three frame sizes to choose from.

The V3’s design was updated with many new features, including a now-removable battery for even more convenient charging both on and off the bike. And with a sub-40 lb weight, it’s hard to beat this one on a pound-for-pound basis!

Check out my full review of the Ride1Up Roadster V3 here:

Lectric Ebikes expanded into larger territory with the launch of the Lectric XPeak2. It was the company’s first e-bike with a larger than 20″ wheel size, bringing some chunky 26×4.0″ tires into the company’s inventory.

The XPeak takes the form of a typical adventure-style fat tire electric bike. It features a hardtail design with a suspension fork, high-volume tires, hydraulic disc brakes, and a relatively large battery that is certified by the Swiss testing agency SGS to meet UL 2849 standards.

The bike is also tested to higher standards for ruggedness of the frame and fork than most e-bikes in this price range, meaning it’s likely going to last longer too. As far as fat tire adventure-style e-bikes go, there’s no better bang for your buck than the Lectric XPeak2.

The $2,295 Ride1Up Prodigy V2 Eco has brought new meaning to the phrase “low-cost mid-drive electric bike”, updating an already impressive e-bike with a new next-gen setup.

This e-bike sports a high-end German-manufactured Brose mid-drive motor and can hit speeds of up to 28 mph (45 km/h), yet is price similarly to much cheaper hub motor-based electric bikes.

It’s a perfect commuter e-bike for someone who wants high-quality parts like that German mid-drive motor with 90Nm of torque, hydraulic disc brakes, bright LED lights, included fenders, rear rack, and a very comfortable saddle. Plus, there’s a choice between a step-over or a mid-step frame to better fit a wider variety of riders.

This e-bike is easily worth another $700 more than the price tag, if not more. In fact, you’ll find many of these same components on e-bikes with $2,000-$3,000 price tags all over the place. So if you’re looking for a higher quality e-bike that doesn’t cost as much as a fancy bike shop e-bike, this is the one to consider.

The Ride1Up Vorsa is Ride1Up’s most versatile “SUV‑style” e‑bike yet, designed to transition seamlessly from commuting through city streets to loaded errands and light gravel or park paths. With a standard price around US $1,495, it delivers an exceptionally high‑value riding experience that often outperforms models far above its price range.

With a powerful 750 W rear‑hub motor producing a hefty 95 Nm of torque (and peaking over 1,400 W in real-world tests), the Vorsa accelerates comfortably and can maintain Class 3 speeds of up to 28 mph via pedal assist or 20 mph throttle thanks to its dual-sensor system and customizable display. Whether you’re hauling cargo or cruising commute lanes, the Vorsa delivers smooth, responsive power that feels natural and planted.

I love how well‑rounded the package is. Ride1Up outfits the Vorsa with a 100 mm suspension fork, comfortable ergonomic bars, multiple colors/frame styles (step‑thru or standard), and a modular rear rack rated for 440 lb total payload, which is expandable via Connect+ accessories. Hydraulic disc brakes, full fenders, integrated lighting, and passive features like UL‑certified batteries and Apple FindMy integration round out the build.

At just $1,695, the Ride1Up Vorsa is priced way below fair market value, which makes this an absolute steal of a deal.

Check out my Ride1Up Vorsa review here, and also check out the new expansion to the Vorsa line with lighter and heavier duty models built on the Vorsa platform.

The Marker is the first model unveiled by the e-bike brand spun out of Lectric eBikes, known as Monarc. The brand leverages Lectric’s size and buying power to bring higher end bikes than we typically see in the budget space – complete with higher shelf components – but at better prices and with better customer service.

The Monarc Marker is a powerful, Class 3-capable fat tire adventure e-bike with powerful four-piston brakes, a quality reverse arch suspension fork, Shimano CUES transmission, and comes with two batteries, all for an incredible reasonable MSRP of US $1,999.

Monarc also made waves by offering a 5-year warranty as well as 12-hour customer service five days a week, ensuring customers feel comfortable knowing they are buying from a company that will stick around to support riders long after the purchase is complete.

Learn more about the Monarc Marker here, or check out its current sale price here.

When Juiced returned with a vengeance after being saved by Lectric, the brand did so with two impressive new Scrambler models: a hard tail and a full-suspension bike. They’re both awesome moped-style e-bikes and handle great, but the full-suspension version definitely kicks things up a notch and allows for even wilder riding.

But since they share the same features and performance outside the rear suspension, either would make an awesome addition to your e-bike fleet. Basically, it’s a powerful yet street-legal e-bike that is designed for real-world riding, thanks in no small part to its designer, Austin Homes, being a seasoned competitive bike racer across multiple disciplines and bringing that keen eye to the Scrambler’s development.

By the time you surpass the $2,000 mark, you’re starting to get into serious e-bikes with serious performance or build quality.

If you’re spending this much, you’ll want to ensure you’re buying from a reputable company with top-notch products and service.

The Ride1Up TraiRush is the first actually quite good electric mountain bike that comes in at a budget price. It gives you a real German-made Brose motor, which offers good power matched with whisper-quiet performance. You get a dropper seat post and Maxxis Minion tires, Tektro Orion quad-piston hydraulic disc brakes, and a 120mm Rockshox Judy Silver TK Air Fork.

The first time I took a ride on the Ride1Up TrailRush, I couldn’t believe just how good the bike felt, especially from a company known for its wide array of commuter and street-oriented electric bikes. But Ride1Up was founded by cyclists at heart, and so they know what it means to build a good bike, regardless of category. At $2,095, this is a steal for just great parts.

I love the $3,299 Priority Current electric bike so much that it has become one of my daily riders. It’s the one I grab when I’m doing city riding and I know I want to be pedaling.

While the original version was already a great bike, the new Current Plus adds some key improvements, such as an included throttle and a nearly 50% larger capacity battery for more range. The throttle is great in a pinch, but the bike is also just so much fun to pedal thanks to a torque sensor-based pedal assist that provides natural-feeling assistance up to a fast 28 mph top speed.

The internally geared rear hub combined with a Gates belt drive system and powerful hydraulic disc brakes make this a very nice e-bike for the price. Plus that mid-drive motor offers tons of torque (140 Nm!) for climbing hills or accelerating quickly.

I’m also a fan of the built-in lights and included fenders, not to mention the punchy hydraulic disc brakes and Goodyear tires.

Mid-drive electric bikes aren’t cheap, but the price is definitely a fair one for this e-bike.

I’m not normally a carbon fiber, drop bar, race bike kind of guy. But then again, that’s usually because those types of bikes are way out of my price range… until now.

The Ride1Up CF Racer1 brings carbon fiber road e-bikes to the common man, offering both a traditional road bike and a gravel bike edition.

At under 29 lb (13 kg), these e-bikes are some of the lightest around. I picked one up with my index finger and was shocked at how easily it left the ground.

To get there, that meant a relatively low-power motor and a smaller battery were required. But for the folks that ride these types of bikes, a big motor and heavy battery probably aren’t on their wishlist anyway. Instead, a simple extra boost, as if you’ve always got a tailwind, helps augment your own pedaling.

For just $2,295, there’s no better deal out there for a budget-friendly carbon fiber road e-bike.

This full-suspension electric moped-style e-bike is a masterclass in high performance electric bike design. Sure, it’s an obvious SUPER73 imitation, but it’s a really well done imitation.

It handles well, it’s got great rubber between you and the road, the suspension gives a comfortable ride, and it’s got plenty of extra power.

Despite the 750W label, the e-bike can be unlocked to make use of nearly 1,500W of peak power. I was able to reach a top speed of 37 mph (59.5 km/h) during my testing (not on public streets!).

But the bike ships in Class 2 mode with a 750W limit and 20 mph (32 km/h) maximum speed, meaning you’ll have to contact the company to request an unlock for the bike to be used above Class 2 speeds and power levels.

All in all, the $2,395 Ride1Up REVV1 FS is an amazing e-bike for anyone who wants a moped-style ride with full-suspension, high quality 4-piston hydraulic disc brakes and a design that rides almost like a motorcycle, yet in a bike form factor.

Check out my complete Ride1Up REVV1 FS review here.

The Tern Quick Haul is an electric cargo bike from the famed cargo e-bike company Tern. It holds the distinction of being the most affordable model in Tern’s lineup.

That says a lot, because Tern is known for its high-quality e-bikes that are built to be ridden every day for real-world errands, carpool duty, and more. These e-bikes are meant to replace cars, and they do just that for many riders. With a huge list of accessories, you can outfit a Quick Haul for just about any job.

The Tern Quick Haul features a mid-drive Bosch motor, hydraulic disc brakes, Tern’s vertical parking rack, and a relatively lightweight but high utility design that is meant to carry just about anything in your life.

We had the chance to test a pair of Quick Hauls at Eurobike in Frankfurt last year, which you can learn more about here.

Motor: Bosch Active Line Plus mid-drive motorTop speed: 20 mph (32 km/h) with pedal assist (no throttle)Range: Up to 60 miles (96 km), less when riding in highest powerBattery: 400Wh, removable/lockableWeight: 50 lb (22.8 kg)Price: $2,999

When it comes to fast, powerful electric mopeds on a budget, it’s hard to compete with the $2,399 Ariel Rider X-Class. This is the e-bike I usually recommend when someone simply wants the most power and speed for the least amount of money.

The impressive performance comes from a powerful 1,000W motor that actually puts out closer to 2,000W of peak power. It can be limited to 20 mph if you’d like, but I got it up to 36 mph in unlimited mode.

The bike rolls on 20″ moto-style wheels and comes with nice parts including hydraulic disc brakes, a bench seat, dual suspension, and a big moto-style headlight.

You can see my full review of the Ariel Rider 52V X-Class here.

The Xtracycle Hopper is something of a Goldilocks cargo bike, in my opinion. It’s not as big as a traditional long tail cargo bike, but it’s still big enough to carry multiple kids or a week’s worth of groceries.

It’s also not as pricey as the expensive $5-7k bike shop cargo e-bikes, yet it’s much higher quality than the typical direct-to-consumer cargo e-bikes.

I’ve tested it for all sorts of cargo jobs and even put my friends and family on it to take their kids to school and let me know how it works for them. Everyone has come away impressed with the ride feel, which seems much more like a traditional bike than a bulky cargo bike.

For the money, the Xtracycle Hopper offers incredible value and much higher quality construction than you’d get with entry-level brands.

Check out my full Xtracycle Hopper review here.

Above $3.5k, you’re entering some seriously dedicated e-bike territory.

These e-bikes either feature top-shelf components like drivetrain and brake parts, or pack in so much power that they couldn’t possibly be priced any lower.

Either way, you’re not in Kansas anymore. You’re heading down the e-bike rabbit hole.

Tern is renowned for building high-quality electric bikes that double down on utility features. The newly updated Tern HSD is the company’s more affordable utility e-bike, offering many features you’ll find on its higher-end GSD but at a price that more riders can stomach.

The HSD uses small 20″ wheels and includes a folding stem, making it nice and compact. But taking it one step further, Tern outfitted it with its innovative rack that lets you park the bike on its tail, meaning it takes up barely more space than a coat rack in the corner of a room.

The bike’s Bosch mid-drive and high capacity batteries provide a high-end e-bike drive, and they are matched with a variety of performance drivetrain options that you can select from, depending on how high you want to spec the bike.

Whether you’re ferrying around kids or buying a week’s worth of groceries, the HSD is a solid option.

Check out my detailed first ride experience on the Tern HSD here.

The Xtracycle Swoop ASM is one of those electric cargo bikes that makes you rethink the entire category.

Not only does it include high-quality components and fancy features like automatic shifting paired with a high-end Shimano mid-drive motor, but it also just feels like you’re riding a normal-sized bike instead of a long and awkward cargo e-bike.

I found I can even ride it without my hands on the bars because it tracks so straight and rides so stable. That’s a feat you can’t pull off with most budget cargo e-bikes since they aren’t designed with proper bicycle geometry. But Xtracycle is a true cyclist’s cargo e-bike company, and so they understand proper bicycle design.

Check out my full review of this incredibly high-end and comfortable-to-ride cargo e-bike here.

When it comes to all-wheel-drive electric bikes, the $3,299 Ariel Rider Grizzly is one of my favorites.

This incredible e-bike is really more of a small electric motorcycle. It uses two high-power motors – one in each wheel – to offer speeds in the mid-30s and acceleration that will get you there in the blink of an eye.

Full suspension and hydraulic disc brakes make this an e-bike that handles well at any speed, and the bright red paint job looks as aggressive as the e-bike feels. It even comes with footpegs pre-installed so you can carry a passenger with you.

Plus it’s got double batteries to feed those two power-hungry motors, so you’ll be able to ride this e-bike pretty darn far too, as long as you aren’t too demanding from the throttle.

Check out my full in-depth review of the epic Ariel Rider Grizzly e-bike here.

The Priority Skyline builds upon Priority Bicycles’ legacy of creating high-performance, low-maintenance e-bikes and ups the ante with more premium parts than we’ve ever seen on a Priority e-bike before.

The show stopper here is the inclusion of Pinion’s 12-speed automatic gearbox, which smartly shifts gears without the rider ever having to think about it. You can personalize your shift points to dial in the ride, and let the centrally-mounted gearbox do the rest. The carbon belt drive transfers your pedal power while a 750W rear hub motor brings the extra power riders need.

There are also high-quality hydraulic brakes, a great suspension fork, and a big battery for long range in the city. Rear rack and complete fenders come standard, as do the built-in LED lights and adjustable handle-bar stem.

See my full review of the Priority Bicycles Skyline here.

You just couldn’t stop, could you? I showed you plenty of awesome e-bikes that didn’t cost most of a paycheck. But you just haaaaaaad to keep going.

Fine, here they are. These are some of the most expensive e-bikes out there that we’ve tested and that I’d actually recommend someone buying, if you can pony up the cash.

When it comes to ultra-premium folding electric bikes, there’s only one name that comes to my mind: GoCycle. These are the most impressive folding e-bikes I’ve ever seen. And they better be – they were designed by a former McLaren engineer.

Between the carbon fiber work, single-sided wheels, high-tech tires, trippy LED dot display, innovative rear suspension, and completely enclosed drivetrain, there’s a lot of high-end stuff going on here.

The speed will vary from 15-20 mph depending on which country you buy the bike in, but the performance is only part of the equation. The quick folding and light, compact design of the e-bike is the real winner here.

Check out my full review of the GoCycle G4i+ here.

The FREY EX Pro is a high-end electric mountain bike with crazy high power. It uses the same motor as the CSC FT1000MD we featured above (the Bafang M620 mid-drive), but cranks up the current to provide closer to 1,500W of power.

It’s enough to destroy chains if you aren’t careful. We know. Several of us here at Electrek have done it.

The FREY EX Pro not only features high-end parts like a RockShox Lyric fork and Magura MT5e hydraulic brakes, but it even comes with dual batteries so you can ride farther and stay on the trails longer.

I’ve personally taken this e-bike back UP a downhill mountain bike course – that’s how powerful it is.

It may be expensive, but it saves you several thousand dollars compared to many European electric mountain bikes with similar components but a fraction of the power in the FREY.

See my full review of the FREY EX Pro here.

The Tern GSD improves upon the Tern HSD with even more capability and even nicer components. There are multiple versions that can take you up to nearly $10k, but even the lower-tier versions are not “low tier,” if you know what I mean.

Parts like automatically engaging electric locks, 10-speed Shimano Deore transmissions, Magura MT5e quad-piston hydraulic disc brakes, and more set these e-bikes apart from the rest of the pack.

If you’re getting a Tern GSD, you’re getting a fully qualified car replacement. There are many people who use these instead of buying a second car. When you look at it like that, the price suddenly seems pretty reasonable.
